		<title>The Semiconductor Singularity</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/</link>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Accelerating IC innovation with AI.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/">The Semiconductor Singularity</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Semiconductor innovation is entering a new era defined by exponential feedback loops between hardware and software, a transformation Ray Kurzweil characterizes as the “semiconductor singularity.” While AI is rapidly dismantling long-standing bottlenecks in IC design and verification, true acceleration requires secure, explainable and trustworthy solutions from design through manufacturing. This paper explains how agentic AI systems, such as Siemens Calibre AI, enable rapid, auditable workflows, foster cross-team and cross-company collaboration and embed transparency and trust into every feedback loop. As the industry approaches the singularity, embedding trust becomes as vital as technical advancement, ensuring sustainable, scalable semiconductor progress.</p>
<h2>What you&#8217;ll learn:</h2>
<ul>
<li>How agentic AI removes verification bottlenecks in semiconductor workflows</li>
<li>Why trust and transparency are crucial for sustainable innovation</li>
<li>The future of collaborative IC signoff enabled by secure AI platforms</li>
</ul>
<h2>Who should read this:</h2>
<ul>
<li>Semiconductor executives and digital transformation leaders</li>
<li>IC design and verification managers seeking workflow acceleration</li>
<li>EDA technology strategists and engineers</li>
<li>Business decision-makers focused on secure innovation</li>
</ul>
<p>To read more, click <a href="https://resources.sw.siemens.com/en-US/white-paper-the-semiconductor-singularity-accelerating-ic-innovation-with-ai/">here</a>.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity/">The Semiconductor Singularity</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Rethinking Memory For AI Inference</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-memory-for-ai-inference/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-memory-for-ai-inference/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Liz Allan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:11:46 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Arteris]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Ayar Labs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Baya Systems]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[Expedera]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[Keysight EDA]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[near-memory]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[processing in memory]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[processing-near-memory]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Processing data closer to memory could reduce power and boost performance, but software, thermal, and architectural challenges remain.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/rethinking-memory-for-ai-inference/">Rethinking Memory For AI Inference</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Moving compute closer to memory can reduce data movement, power consumption, and latency.</li>
<li>Different AI workloads require different approaches to memory architecture, interconnects, and processing.</li>
<li>Software support, standardization, and thermal management remain major barriers to wider adoption.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>The shift from AI training in data centers to AI inferencing at the edge will require a fundamental rethinking of where and how data is processed and stored, how it is prioritized, and what needs to be traded off in terms of power and performance to stay within a limited power budget.</p>
<p>At the center of these efforts is a recognition that the explosion of data — especially in physical AI where data is being live-streamed from sensors — requires much of that data to be processed as close to memory as possible. This has been a topic of discussion since the inception of the Internet of Things, but it really began gaining traction with AI due to the flood of data and concerns about the amount of power required to move and process it. In battery-electric vehicles and drones, for example, the less energy spent on processing data, the greater the range per charge and the more energy that can be allocated to critical and not-so-critical functions.</p>
<p>The challenge is understanding what data needs to be kept, what can be discarded, and what needs to be acted upon. Those are very different priorities. Making those choices requires advanced processing, and the quickest path to results using the least amount of energy is to minimize the distance data needs to travel between processing and memory.</p>
<p>&#8220;The realities of these LLMs getting into physical devices are causing everyone to rethink how they architect their memory,&#8221; said Frank Ferro, group director for product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cadence-design-systems/">Cadence</a>. &#8220;Even with conventional memory — without even talking about processing in memory — how do I partition these models to maximize the performance both of the overall processor and memory subsystem?&#8221;</p>
<p>Solving those issues will require several new approaches, such as processing near memory (PNM) and processing in memory (PIM), as well as improvements across multiple technologies and approaches. Compilers and operating systems will need to catch up, and standards will need to be established.</p>
<p>&#8220;AI in the data center and all these large language models have been the focus of everyone&#8217;s attention for the last couple of years, and now these models have been trained and pushed out into the field, into the edge of the network and physical AI,&#8221; Ferro said. &#8220;The reality is you have limited compute power and limited memory.&#8221;</p>
<p>AI inferencing at the edge presents different challenges than training AI models in AI data centers.</p>
<p>&#8220;Training almost always involves highly variable memory access patterns, and mostly involves the use of interpreted code — for example, Python to PyTorch — which necessitates the complexities of coherence across layers of memory,&#8221; said Steve Roddy, chief marketing officer at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/quadric/">Quadric</a>. &#8220;Once inference begins, the use model changes dramatically. Whether you are looking at big LLMs running in a data center, or models running locally, such as smaller LLMs or VLAs (vision-language-action) in cars, the model is known before you run it. That means the data access patterns are known ahead of time. This is where ahead-of-time compilation can start to take great advantage of the various at-memory, processing-in-memory, or in-memory-compute (IMC) options. The system knows exactly where, when, and how the compute is going to occur. There is no need for one element of a compute solution to guess if something manipulated the data while it was in another memory subsystem, because the entire system orchestration was planned out hours, days, or weeks before, when the AI inference model was compiled.&#8221;</p>
<p>Certain stages of the AI inference pipeline benefit from near-memory solutions more than others.</p>
<p>&#8220;Pushing the compute into the DRAM or near the DRAM has interesting implications,&#8221; said Sharad Chole, chief scientist and co-founder of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/expedera/">Expedera</a>. &#8220;You&#8217;re moving your communication pattern — my activation goes to memory, rather than my memory goes to activation — and that changes how much communication you need to do. These sort of solutions are okay for low-batch decode. For example, if you have batch one and you are trying to do decode and you are not reading all the weights, as soon as we move to large-batch decode, which is cost-optimized, or prefill, which is large-batch execution, the activation communication becomes the challenging part. These are the two ways to think about it. In the end, you have to break your execution into parts that are either storage-sensitive, in terms of how much data you need to store, or bandwidth-sensitive, or batching-sensitive.&#8221;</p>
<p>Either way, bringing compute closer to data can reduce the time and the energy spent moving it, and processing in the same memory package (custom HBM), or on the same memory die (LPDDR6-PIM), provides two fundamental advantages. &#8220;One of them is performance,&#8221; said Kos Gitchev, product marketing director at Cadence. &#8220;If we&#8217;re looking at a CPU or a core sitting inside the SoC, and every time you need to do an operation you&#8217;ve got to go out to memory, there&#8217;s a transaction that says, &#8216;Okay, get me some data from the memory that&#8217;s going to hit a cache somewhere in the SoC.&#8217; That will eventually go to memory. It will come back to the cache and eventually come back to the processor. This operation really takes some time.&#8221;</p>
<p>So while chip architects can get compute running quickly on an SoC, the SoC has to wait for data to arrive. &#8220;Processing in memory to a large degree addresses this,&#8221; said Gitchev. &#8220;Now you can say, &#8216;I have this chunk of memory data in the memory, go do something with it, and I&#8217;m just going to go read the results. I&#8217;m going to skip this whole step of reading it, compute again, and putting it back. I&#8217;m just going to do a read, and that&#8217;s all I need.'&#8221;</p>

</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
</div>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Compute-in-memory vs. processing-in/near-memory — where the math happens and what each term means. Note that CIM and IMC are used interchangeably in hardware to describe architectures that perform computational tasks directly within the memory array, but IMC also has a separate software meaning. Source: Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p><strong>Reducing data movement</strong><br />
Ultimately, the entire system needs to be optimized to make this scheme work.</p>
<p>“The framing has shifted from, &#8216;We need more memory,&#8217; to, &#8216;We need fabric that keeps memory fed without starving other traffic,'&#8221; said Nandan Nayampally, chief commercial officer at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/baya-systems/">Baya Systems</a>. &#8220;More recently it&#8217;s, &#8216;How to architect around scarcity itself.'&#8221;</p>
<p>Others agree. &#8220;AI systems are increasingly limited by the ability to move data efficiently between compute, memory, accelerators, chiplets, and dies,&#8221; said Andy Nightingale, vice president of product management and marketing, at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/arterisip/">Arteris</a>. &#8220;This makes the interconnect and memory architecture as important as the memory device itself. This reinforces the need for scalable NoC, chiplet, cache coherence, and system mapping technologies that help customers optimize data movement before silicon.&#8221;</p>
<p>Interconnects, caching schemes, and packaging all play increasingly significant roles at the edge, but choosing the right combination while also keeping costs down is a challenge. So while new versions of high-bandwidth memory are enabling more processing in the base die of a multi-die stack, an LPDDR6-PIM that places slices of compute alongside the DRAM banks may be more attractive, depending on the cost.</p>
<p>&#8220;Near-memory computing is still using an external channel interface,&#8221; said Hoshik Kim, senior vice president and fellow at SK hynix.[1] &#8220;But by putting processing units near memory data, we can actually get rid of or remove a lot of data movement and have higher energy efficiency. Also, we can offload the compute function from the CPU or GPU, so that we can have better utilization of the host CPU or GPU.&#8221;</p>
<p>Typical use cases involve memory controllers inside the base die. &#8220;We are integrating some useful near-memory processing units to offload simple MAC operations,&#8221; said Kim. &#8220;Because, if you think about inference workload operations, most function computation is a simple MAC operation. So we could put MAC operations inside the base die to expand for and then work with the GPU for a more complex, compute-intensive operation.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Multi-year process</strong><br />
Some of the necessary technologies are available today. Others will take years to develop, or improve enough to the point where they can make a difference.</p>
<p>&#8220;We are looking at in-memory applications in three to five years, maybe around 2030,&#8221; said Vishal Chandrasekar, director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/ayar-labs/">Ayar Labs</a>. &#8220;You want to be able to connect up many GPUs so that you are able to have different GPUs access the memory of others. People are trying to ramp up the amount of memory per GPU, but you&#8217;re limited by packet size and HBM bandwidths and the like. Compute in memory is one path, which makes it so the base die of your memory now has compute capabilities of some kind.&#8221;</p>
<p>Still, that technology is not quite ready for commercialization. &#8220;It&#8217;s a little aggressive to say that we&#8217;re already seeing it out in the market,&#8221; said Cadence&#8217;s Ferro. &#8220;There might be some very specific embedded applications that are using a type of processing in memory, but as a broad usage, it&#8217;s still pretty early.&#8221;</p>
<p>Processing-in-memory solutions are slowly moving from research to commercialization. For example, Samsung <a href="https://semiconductor.samsung.com/news-events/tech-blog/samsung-lpddr6-advancing-low-power-memory-for-ai-with-industry-first-validation-on-qualcomm-technologies-new-snapdragon-platform/">announced</a> the architecture and performance benchmarks for its LPDDR6-PIM chip aimed at low-power, mobile on-device AI applications.</p>
<p>&#8220;LPDDR-PIM is the one that has got the most attention within JEDEC,&#8221; said Randy White, memory solutions program manager at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/keysight-technologies/">Keysight</a>. &#8220;There is active development and discussion around LPDDR-PIM, but there are challenges for both the hardware and software. From a hardware standpoint, you&#8217;re mixing logic process with DRAM process, and they&#8217;re optimized for different metrics, so that innovation itself makes the manufacturing process more complex and expensive. You have to be very selective and make sure you know your application can sustain the added cost of that.&#8221; Applications for LPDDR6-PIM will range from fixed-node edge servers optimized for performance to mobile phones and tablets optimized for efficiency.</p>
<p><strong>Trickier than hardware?</strong><br />
The software challenges may be even more difficult. &#8220;How do you manage workload skew and load balancing?&#8221; White noted. &#8220;Let&#8217;s say you take a checkerboard-style memory array or core, and every 10 columns or so of memory, you stick a vertical slice of logic silicon. If you were to take that PIM die, put it under a microscope, you&#8217;d see a checkerboard with slices repeated every certain number of rows. That by itself has some timing constraints. While you&#8217;re accessing one row, you have to wait for calculation to an instruction to be complete on the compute engine. So it&#8217;s not just a matter of fetching data and balancing timings between different bank accesses. Now you have memory access coupled with waiting for compute data. The whole point is that I don&#8217;t have to move data as much. I&#8217;m moving data between adjacent cells, and that&#8217;s great. From a hardware standpoint, you get lower power, etc. But for the engineer who&#8217;s writing the BIOS, who has to create the scheduler for synchronizing, that has just exploded in terms of complexity.&#8221;</p>
<p>PIM architectures need to be considered in terms of the impact on the rest of the system. &#8220;The hardware has to be designed and optimized so you have the right number of compute coefficients and things like that,&#8221; said White. &#8220;Then the memory has to be segregated in a way that you don&#8217;t have too fine resolution, so you have the right coarse and fine trade-offs in terms of separation.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284022" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=1854%2C846&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1854" height="846" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?w=1854&amp;ssl=1 1854w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=300%2C137&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=1024%2C467&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=768%2C350&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=1536%2C701&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/SK-hynix-slide-2.jpeg?resize=600%2C274&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" />Fig. 2: What must be solved for near-data computing to achieve its potential. Source: SK hynix/DAC</strong></p>
<p>Additionally, memory programming models are still in flux, particularly with the rise of non-traditional memory systems such as PIM, CIM, CXL-attached tiered memory, and heterogeneous memory architectures.</p>
<p>&#8220;I don&#8217;t think the industry will settle on a single programming model and coherence implementation,&#8221; said Steven Woo, fellow and distinguished inventor at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/rambus-inc/">Rambus</a>. &#8220;Over the last 40 years, different programming models, coherence protocols, and consistency models have been implemented that provide different trade-offs to systems designers and application developers. The diversity of architectures and coherence implementations is driving the need for more high-performance libraries to be developed that allow programmers to call common functions that are implemented in ways that are optimized for different systems. High-performance libraries maintain a level of abstraction that improves program portability and performance scalability without burdening the programmer with understanding all details of the underlying hardware.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Compiler and memory controller considerations</strong><br />
Software innovations are needed to support new memory architectures.</p>
<p>&#8220;The question is no longer whether a PIM is viable or makes sense,&#8221; said SK hynix&#8217;s Kim. &#8220;It&#8217;s a reality. The problem is how to make a software compiler framework and system software. All of the ecosystem needs to follow up.&#8221;</p>
<p>Cadence&#8217;s Gitchev agreed. &#8220;The real challenge is that currently the systems are designed so that the compilers, operating system, and everything works with a processor core or an accelerator sitting inside your SoC, and everything is designed for this processor to do all the work,&#8221; he said. &#8220;That&#8217;s the infrastructure. Now, essentially, you can offload some of that processor work and say, &#8216;I need to do this operation, and I don&#8217;t really need the processor to do it.&#8217; There&#8217;s a whole infrastructure behind that to be able to do that. There&#8217;s a software aspect. The operating system and the processor need to understand, &#8216;These operations I can send to memory, they&#8217;ll do it, and I&#8217;ll just get the results, but these operations I still have to do locally because the memory I have may not be able to do it, or my system is not as efficient.'&#8221;</p>
<p>There&#8217;s also the need for standardization inside the chip. &#8220;How do you connect these things together?&#8221; said Gitchev. &#8220;Today we don&#8217;t really have a good way to say, &#8216;Go and do this operation in memory,&#8217; as far as the type of transactions that move from the processor to the SoC fabric to memory. This infrastructure is not there. We need the hardware and protocol to be able to tell memory to go do this stuff, and then we need the software and the operating system to support the system to be able to do this and decide what&#8217;s efficient.&#8221;</p>
<p>This comes down to two streams of data. &#8220;I&#8217;ve got a stream of data that&#8217;s storing and reading and writing,&#8221; explained Ferro. &#8220;Then I&#8217;ve got a stream of data that&#8217;s compute, and that&#8217;s not native to the way operating systems and processors are built. You now need cooperation with the processor companies, the interface between the processor and the fabric, the system, and then of course the operating system. All those pieces need to get adjusted. It may or may not be a big adjustment, but without standardization, everyone&#8217;s system is going to be highly customized.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Home agents and memory controllers</strong><br />
Near-memory and in-memory architectures introduce distinct pressures to traffic management and system coherence.</p>
<p>&#8220;In traditional hardware coherent systems, home agents are responsible for tracking where shared data is being used throughout the system and making sure processing elements have correct copies of the data,&#8221; explained Rambus&#8217; Woo. &#8220;Hardware coherency eliminates the need for programmers to manage the updating of data across processing elements, simplifying programming. Synchronization primitives like locks and barriers, and software coherence give applications an additional level of control over how and when data is updated. Together, these hardware and software mechanisms enable efficient algorithms to be designed that maintain a level of hardware abstraction that hides the details of the underlying hardware, easing the task of programming and managing the way data is updated to maintain coherence in systems.&#8221;</p>
<p>That means software engineers increasingly need to understand the hardware, and vice versa.</p>
<p>&#8220;Achieving good, scalable performance as problem sizes grow and the number of processing elements increases is becoming more difficult,&#8221; said Woo. &#8220;While home agents remove the burden of managing coherence from the programmer, the messages sent by home agents to maintain coherence can become performance bottlenecks. Programmers increasingly need to understand the underlying structure and functions of the hardware to achieve good performance. Algorithms are often modified to shard data in ways that maximize the independence of tasks and minimize the amount of coherence traffic traveling in the system. In some systems, utilizing no hardware coherence mechanisms results in the best performance, but complicates the task of programming.&#8221;</p>
<p>The memory controller faces a twofold challenge. &#8220;One part is the memory subsystem, which consists of a PHY and a controller,&#8221; said Cadence&#8217;s Gitchev. &#8220;The controller itself is where these commands need to actually do the work. But now you have a different set of commands. Before that, you fundamentally had two different commands — read data, write data, and that&#8217;s about it. The control is a lot more complicated than that, but at a high level, that&#8217;s what it does. It reads data and writes data in the most efficient way possible. Now a different challenge is coming in where we need a different set of commands and rules to say, &#8216;Hey, now can you go take this data and multiply it, and take this data inside the memory and divide it, or do an operation.&#8217; It&#8217;s not a significant challenge for a controller to do that. We add more commands, and we&#8217;ll figure out how that works, and try to make it efficient.&#8221;</p>
<p>The second challenge is harder. &#8220;The processor needs to be able to send those commands,&#8221; said Gitchev. &#8220;It needs to prepare a memory inside the memory subsystem to say, &#8216;If I&#8217;m going to multiply this data by that data, do I have the right data in the right place? Do I have the results in the right place? This operation is complete.&#8217; The controller manages some of that, but it also has to have very close collaboration with the overall system.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Thermal, physics, and CIM</strong><br />
Processing near memory can help reduce data traffic, but it could amplify existing physics problems, such as thermal issues.</p>
<p>&#8220;The memory shortage is partially due to current state-of-the-art design and use of available memory,&#8221; said Satish Radhakrishnan, head of global field applications and solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/vinci/">Vinci</a>. &#8220;Innovative design can help reduce demand per unit and improve utilization. The most viable solutions to increasing memory utilization — including higher HBM stacks, custom HBM, near-memory, in-memory compute, and chiplet-based disaggregation — all share one property. They trade a bandwidth problem for a physics problem. Stacking memory closer to compute concentrates heat in exactly the places it&#8217;s hardest to remove. Heterogeneous material stacks and micron-scale interconnects create thermo-mechanical stress and warpage behavior that doesn&#8217;t respect the boundaries between die, package, and board. Heat doesn&#8217;t stop at the chip, and neither do the constraints that come with it.&#8221;</p>
<p>For this reason, memory companies tend to announce new thermal technology with their new architectures. For example, SK hynix announced an integrated HBM (iHBM) solution that embeds <a href="https://news.skhynix.com/en/ihbm-solution/">integrated cooling elements</a> (ICEs) within the HBM package. Micron&#8217;s recent <a href="https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_downloads/2026/10/MU-TP-Webinar.pdf">roadmap</a> included vertical HBM (vHBM) with thermally aware design and a heat spreader over the PHY. Samsung is tackling heat issues with a range of solutions, including <a href="https://news.samsung.com/global/samsung-unveils-hbm4e-showcasing-comprehensive-ai-solutions-nvidia-partnership-and-vision-at-nvidia-gtc-2026">hybrid copper bonding</a> (HCB) and a <a href="https://semiconductor.samsung.com/news-events/tech-blog/introducing-a-new-package-architecture-for-improved-thermal-efficiency-in-mobile-application-processors/">heat path block</a> (HPB), which it plans to include in HBM5. [2]</p>
<p>SK hynix&#8217;s Kim proposed in-memory compute to reduce heat in the HBM base die. &#8220;If you put DRAM on top of logic, DRAM is very sensitive to temperature,&#8221; he said. &#8220;Logic is very prone to hot spots. Basically, the hot spots happen at the bottom of memory. Thermal can be a real challenge, and you need to resolve these issues. In-memory computing can be an answer. It has extremely low power consumption, so you can probably put in-memory computing at the base die or the bottom of memory.&#8221;</p>
<p>In practice, IMC/CIM could work with custom HBM4 or HBM4E because the base die is built on a foundry logic process. SRAM is the native memory, meaning that SRAM CIM macros can be built there. CIM does multiply-accumulate inside the SRAM array, so weights aren’t repeatedly moved between memory and a separate MAC unit. Data movement is where most of the energy goes, which is why SRAM CIM macros report far better energy efficiency, in operations per watt, than conventional digital accelerators. Less energy per operation means lower power density, so fewer hot spots under the DRAM.</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
Software support remains the missing link between near-memory hardware and its promise for AI. For compilers and AI frameworks, processing-near-memory and in-memory architectures are emerging targets that could let developers offload mathematical operators directly to the memory subsystem, but the software needed to support that shift is still catching up.</p>
<p>The available compute — and the limits on it — differ by architecture. LPDDR6-PIM is limited to MAC units or simple ALUs suited to matrix-vector multiplication and element-wise math. A custom HBM4/HBM4E logic-process base die can add other memory-control functions. But that flexibility comes with a thermal ceiling — how much compute fits depends on the heat it adds beneath the DRAM.</p>
<p>&#8220;We have spent a lot of time over the last two years talking about custom HBM and how to use that base layer,&#8221; noted Cadence&#8217;s Ferro.</p>
<p>AI is driving the push to bring compute closer to memory, but proximity alone will not deliver the gains. Broader adoption depends on matching workloads to the hardware, managing heat, and giving compilers and operating systems the tools to decide what to offload. Moving the compute is only part of the solution; making the system use it efficiently is the next test.</p>
<p><strong>References</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>DAC 2026 Research Special Session: &#8220;<a href="https://63dac.conference-program.com/presentation/?id=SSSN120&amp;sess=sess254">Near-Memory Processing is Becoming a Reality</a>&#8221; (SK hynix)</li>
<li><a href="https://www.koreatimes.co.kr/business/tech-science/20260604/cooling-design-emerges-as-battleground-for-next-gen-ai-memory">Cooling design</a> emerges as battleground for next-gen AI memory (Korea Times)</li>
</ol>

There is no one-size-fits-all — yet.</p>

		<title>Beyond Bandwidth: AI Inference Is Reshaping The Memory Wall</title>

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kushal Koolwal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:08:39 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[A Bit About Memory]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Longer contexts, higher concurrency, multimodal models, and AI agents will continue changing where memory pressure appears. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/beyond-bandwidth-ai-inference-is-reshaping-the-memory-wall/">Beyond Bandwidth: AI Inference Is Reshaping The Memory Wall</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>AI infrastructure has spent the past several years in a race for compute. More accelerators, larger heterogeneous clusters, and higher peak performance have enabled increasingly capable models. As AI moves into large-scale deployment, infrastructure must support more users, larger models, longer context windows, and more complex applications. Performance still matters, but so do latency, power, utilization, predictability, and cost per inference.</p>
<p>The challenge extends beyond bandwidth. AI systems must balance memory capacity, bandwidth, latency, power, data movement, utilization, and cost. Improving one dimension can create pressure elsewhere. The key-value (KV) cache illustrates this trade-off. KV cache reduces repeated computation during generative AI inference, but requires the system to retain and repeatedly access growing amounts of data. As context lengths and concurrent requests increase, the trade-off becomes more significant.</p>
<p>The result is not simply another memory bottleneck. It is a coordination problem across compute, memory, interconnect, and software. Adding capacity or bandwidth helps only if the system can place, move, and retrieve data without allowing latency, congestion, power, or complexity to erase the benefit.</p>
<h2>The memory wall is getting wider</h2>
<p>The memory wall predates AI. Processor performance historically has improved faster than the ability of memory systems to supply processors with data. AI magnifies that problem. Increasing arithmetic throughput delivers limited benefit if accelerators spend more time waiting for data.</p>
<p>But bandwidth is only part of the constraint. AI systems also need sufficient capacity for models, intermediate data, application context, and inference state. Latency affects responsiveness. Power constrains how much data can be moved economically. Packaging and physical connectivity limit how much high-performance memory can be placed near compute.</p>
<p>These requirements frequently conflict. High-bandwidth memory close to an accelerator can deliver tremendous bandwidth, but its capacity, power, and cost are constrained. Larger-capacity memory may be more economical but may not provide the same bandwidth or latency. Moving data among tiers can expand effective capacity, but consumes bandwidth, power, and time.</p>
<p>The memory wall is therefore becoming a multidimensional system constraint.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284061" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=2479%2C1392&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="2479" height="1392" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?w=2479&amp;ssl=1 2479w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C168&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=1024%2C575&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=768%2C431&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=1536%2C862&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=2048%2C1150&amp;ssl=1 2048w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C337&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: AI scaling turns the memory wall into a system-level trade-off among bandwidth, capacity, latency, power, cost, and utilization.</strong></p>
<p>A system may have substantial aggregate memory, but not all of that capacity is equally useful. Data in a lower tier may technically be available, yet still be too distant, congested, or costly to move within the application’s service-level objective (SLO).</p>
<p>The practical limit is not installed capacity alone. It is <strong>usable capacity within the workload’s latency, bandwidth, and power envelope</strong>.</p>
<h2>Inference changes the economics</h2>
<p>Training large models requires enormous compute resources and memory bandwidth, but it is fundamentally a process of building or updating a model. Inference is dynamic and ongoing. Every prompt, generated token, agent action, and application interaction consumes infrastructure resources.</p>
<p>Inference also is not one workload. A conversational assistant may emphasize latency. An enterprise AI assistant may process substantial proprietary context while supporting many users. AI agents may retain information across multiple steps. Consumer services may prioritize throughput and cost per token. Batch inference may emphasize utilization.</p>
<p>Each creates a different balance among bandwidth, capacity, latency, power, and cost. The architecture must follow the workload.</p>
<h2>KV cache exposes the trade-off</h2>
<p>During transformer inference, attention mechanisms generate key and value representations for tokens in a sequence. Retaining those representations allows subsequent decoding steps to reuse prior states rather than recomputing them for earlier tokens. That improves inference efficiency, but reduces repeated computation while increasing pressure on memory capacity and data movement.</p>
<p>Longer context windows increase the KV-cache footprint of each sequence. Higher concurrency multiplies that requirement across simultaneous requests. An optimization intended to reduce compute demand therefore creates more data that must be retained and accessed.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284062" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=2560%2C1399&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="2560" height="1399" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?w=2560&amp;ssl=1 2560w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=300%2C164&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=1024%2C560&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=768%2C420&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=1536%2C839&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=2048%2C1119&amp;ssl=1 2048w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Beyond-Bandwidth-AI-Infer-Memory-Wall-fig2-scaled.webp?resize=600%2C328&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Reducing repeated computation improves efficiency, but longer contexts and greater concurrency increase the data that must be retained, moved, and managed.</strong></p>
<p>KV state also has a lifecycle. It is created during prefill, repeatedly accessed during decode, and may be retained for reuse. Depending on the serving architecture, it may be moved, replicated, compressed, or evicted. Each phase places different demands on bandwidth, capacity, latency, and control.</p>
<p>KV cache is therefore a data-lifecycle problem, not only a memory allocation. Moving KV state to a lower-cost tier is beneficial only when the higher-tier capacity it frees, or the recomputation it avoids, outweighs transfer latency, bandwidth consumption, energy, and the risk of missing an SLO. In other words, <strong>every movement of data must earn its cost</strong>.</p>
<h2>The workload determines the architecture</h2>
<p>A latency-sensitive assistant may benefit from keeping frequently accessed data close to the accelerator. A long-context enterprise application may emphasize capacity. AI agents may require longer-lived state and more frequent reuse. Large-scale consumer inference may prioritize throughput, utilization, and cost per token.</p>
<p>Frequently accessed data may justify residing near compute. Other data may tolerate additional latency for greater capacity or lower cost, or move among tiers as access patterns change. The objective is not to eliminate the memory hierarchy. It is to coordinate that hierarchy efficiently.</p>
<h2>There is no single memory metric</h2>
<p>Bandwidth remains critical, but optimizing exclusively for bandwidth ignores other constraints. Capacity determines how much model and inference state can remain accessible. Latency affects response time. Power determines whether increasing data movement is sustainable. Utilization indicates whether expensive compute resources are working or waiting for data.</p>
<p>High aggregate throughput can hide unacceptable time to first token, inter-token latency, or tail latency during congestion. A more meaningful measure may be tokens per watt or dollar while meeting latency targets. Headline bandwidth alone does not solve the memory wall.</p>
<p>The relevant question is: <strong>Which combination of compute, memory, interconnect, and software delivers predictable performance within the available power and cost envelope?</strong></p>
<h2>Adding memory is not enough</h2>
<p>High-bandwidth memory can supply data quickly near accelerators. Larger-capacity tiers can complement it where capacity matters more than maximum bandwidth. Expansion and pooling can provide capacity beyond the memory next to an accelerator. Software can place, reuse, compress, move, or discard KV state according to workload requirements.</p>
<p>But every additional tier introduces more decisions. A useful architecture must address four questions:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Placement: </strong>Which data must remain near compute, and which data can tolerate a more distant tier?</li>
<li><strong>Movement: </strong>When is transferring data more efficient than recomputing it?</li>
<li><strong>Coordination: </strong>How should accelerators, memory controllers, interconnects, and software coordinate placement and movement without creating congestion?</li>
<li><strong>Assurance: </strong>How does the system preserve reliability, isolation, data integrity, and predictable performance as inference state crosses additional boundaries?</li>
</ol>
<p>Moving data to a larger-capacity tier may increase effective capacity but add latency. Replicating state may avoid recomputation but consume bandwidth and capacity elsewhere. Pooling may improve utilization, but only if contention and quality of service remain controlled. The advantage comes not from maximizing one memory specification or adding another tier. It comes from coordinating the hierarchy as a system.</p>
<h2>The memory wall will keep moving</h2>
<p>Higher-bandwidth memory, greater capacity, faster interconnects, compression, and smarter cache management can relieve constraints, but not eliminate the challenge. As one bottleneck improves, applications and models use the additional resources. Longer contexts, higher concurrency, multimodal models, and increasingly sophisticated AI agents will continue changing where memory pressure appears. Hardware-software co-design is therefore critical because every layer influences data movement.</p>
<p>The next phase of AI infrastructure scaling will not be defined by compute alone or solved simply by adding memory. The harder task is coordinating where inference data resides, when it moves, and whether moving it is more efficient than recomputing it. The memory wall is becoming something broader: a test of how effectively the system converts distributed memory resources into predictable AI performance.</p>
<h3>Link</h3>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://www.rambus.com/data-center/">Rambus Data Center Chip and IP Solutions</a></li>
</ul>

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Jim Shiely]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:06:49 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Best Of Both: LP & HP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Blogs - LPHP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284121</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Use evidence from silicon to improve the tools and decisions that create the next generation of hardware.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity-when-ai-starts-accelerating-the-silicon-that-accelerates-ai/">The Semiconductor Singularity: When AI Starts Accelerating The Silicon That Accelerates AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>AI is entering the inner loop of semiconductor engineering by reducing the effort required to develop software, operate software tools and carry out engineering analysis.</p>
<p>Ray Kurzweil described the broader pattern as the Law of Accelerating Returns: accumulated technical capability helps create the next computing paradigm. In my recent white paper, <a href="https://resources.sw.siemens.com/en-US/white-paper-the-semiconductor-singularity-accelerating-ic-innovation-with-ai/">The semiconductor singularity: Accelerating IC innovation with AI</a>, I applied that idea to semiconductor engineering. Better computing improves the tools used to design and manufacture semiconductors, and those tools help create the next generation of computing.</p>
<p>Figure 1 places that loop in the longer history of computing. The technologies change, but each generation contributes tools, knowledge and capacity that help create its successor.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284122" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig1-120-yrs-moores-law-drawn.jpg?resize=750%2C385&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="750" height="385" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig1-120-yrs-moores-law-drawn.jpg?w=750&amp;ssl=1 750w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig1-120-yrs-moores-law-drawn.jpg?resize=300%2C154&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig1-120-yrs-moores-law-drawn.jpg?resize=600%2C308&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 750px) 100vw, 750px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Successive computing paradigms have built the technical capacity used to create the next generation.</strong></p>
<h2>Lowering the effort required for the next experiment</h2>
<p>Progress in semiconductor engineering never follows a smooth exponential curve. It advances when a bottleneck is overcome sufficiently for engineers to try something that previously cost too much money, time, computation or organizational effort.</p>
<p>I saw this firsthand with machine learning in computational lithography. Early demonstrations were exciting, but production adoption required developers who understood both ML and semiconductor applications, representative data, model management, IP controls and customer confidence. Production adoption followed only after the model, data, deployment environment and customer requirements matured together.</p>
<p>Generative AI relieves many of the user-complexity bottlenecks characteristic of EDA flows. When I created my original SPIE machine-learning course for lithography, the prototype work took roughly 200 to 300 hours, including some help generating training data. In a later experiment, I used generative AI to recreate and expand a surprising amount of that prototype functionality in roughly 30 hours. These were notebook-level prototypes, not production computational-lithography tools, but the change in exploratory effort was striking.</p>
<p>The practical effect is more engineering exploration within the same schedule. Experienced silicon engineers can examine more alternatives and produce more usable results while spending less time on software and workflow mechanics.</p>
<h2>Extending the reach of engineering expertise</h2>
<p>Agentic AI extends this effect from delivering predictions or analyses to carrying out sequences of engineering work. The LLM interprets intent, assembles the workflow, retrieves relevant context and coordinates specialized tools. Engineering authority remains in the EDA tools, models, qualified data and deterministic analysis.</p>
<p>Semiconductor workflows have traditionally depended on experienced engineers who know which analysis to run, which data matters, when a result is credible and when something requires closer examination. That expertise takes years to develop and cannot expand as quickly as computational capacity or engineering demand.</p>
<p>Agentic systems can increase the reach of that expertise. In computational lithography, an agent might collect recent SEM data, configure model calibration, invoke the appropriate analysis, inspect convergence and runtime, and return exceptions to the engineer for judgment. More computation, data and tool activity can operate under the direction of each expert.</p>
<p>Siemens Fuse EDA AI implements this architecture by connecting AI models to EDA tools, engineering knowledge, specialized languages and data formats.</p>
<h2>Manufacturing connects models to physical evidence</h2>
<p>Computational lithography shows how this works. A fab collects wafer and metrology data, calibrates models, uses those models to drive correction, measures the resulting silicon and feeds what it learns into the next calibration and process change. The model improves through repeated comparison with physical evidence.</p>
<p>Different parts of that loop require different computational methods. Physics-based simulation predicts physical behavior. Qualified models, rule decks and deterministic verification establish engineering and signoff authority. Specialized machine learning supports prediction, classification and optimization. Generative AI improves access to knowledge and tools. Agents coordinate workflows. Accelerated computing expands the analyses that fit within practical schedules.</p>
<p>The result of these workflows is usually the conversion of physical understanding and manufacturing observations into evidence-backed engineering interventions. Simulation directs exploration, metrology records what happened, models reconcile prediction with observation, and engineering action changes the design or process.</p>
<p>As EDA tools, manufacturing systems and digital twins become accessible to agents, this loop can operate across more of the workflow. Agents can explore design-technology tradeoffs, run simulation and computational-metrology flows, evaluate alternatives and organize evidence for the next engineering decision.</p>
<h2>Trust is engineering infrastructure</h2>
<p>As engineering cycles accelerate, the value of trust increases. AI can generate hypotheses, assemble workflows and explore alternatives quickly. Semiconductor development advances through evidence that engineers can responsibly act on: verified designs, qualified models, measured silicon and data whose origin and applicability are understood.</p>
<p>Agentic EDA needs engineering context, secure data access, traceable actions, deterministic authority and human control over consequential decisions. These properties belong inside the workflow and provide a foundation of trust.</p>
<p>A Calibre nmOPC model earns trust through calibration against physical metrology data and continued comparison with what appears on the wafer. The model, calibration data, process conditions and physical evidence together establish where the model can be relied upon. As conditions change, the model is recalibrated and the evidence base grows. AI-enhanced metrology tools such as Canopus can strengthen that foundation by increasing the quantity and quality of SEM data available for calibration.</p>
<p>Calibre, together with foundry-qualified rule decks and verification flows, establishes that a design complies with the physical requirements of manufacturing. Agents can orchestrate more work and explore more possibilities, while trusted engineering tools determine which results are allowed to move forward.</p>
<p>With access to more tools within a well-designed trusted software foundation, engineering analysis may encounter bottlenecks in the larger organization or ecosystem. The time required to establish data access, protect IP or authorize a production change can become the limiting factor on acceleration. Reducing the time required to establish access, protection and authority becomes the next engineering problem.</p>
<p>Figure 2 summarizes how the limiting constraint moves as the engineering workflow accelerates. Physical evidence, deterministic authority, data access, IP protection, validation and human control account for a larger share of the remaining cycle time.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284123" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig2-increasing-automation.jpg?resize=624%2C327&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="624" height="327" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig2-increasing-automation.jpg?w=624&amp;ssl=1 624w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig2-increasing-automation.jpg?resize=300%2C157&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_semi-singularity-AI-fig2-increasing-automation.jpg?resize=600%2C314&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 624px) 100vw, 624px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: As development and analysis accelerate, physical evidence, deterministic authority, data access, IP protection, validation and human control account for more of the remaining end-to-end cycle time.</strong></p>
<h2>The singularity is an accelerating engineering loop</h2>
<p>Better hardware expands AI capability. AI expands what engineers can explore and automate. Manufacturing supplies physical evidence, and verification establishes which results can move forward.</p>
<p>Each improvement changes the limiting constraint. The semiconductor singularity is the continuing acceleration of this engineering loop: identify the next constraint, preserve authority where physics and signoff require it, and use evidence from silicon to improve the tools and decisions that create the next generation of hardware.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-semiconductor-singularity-when-ai-starts-accelerating-the-silicon-that-accelerates-ai/">The Semiconductor Singularity: When AI Starts Accelerating The Silicon That Accelerates AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Designing Edge AI SoCs At Near-Threshold: Why Foundation IP Can Help You Break Down The Power Wall</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/designing-edge-ai-socs-at-near-threshold-why-foundation-ip-can-help-you-break-down-the-power-wall/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/designing-edge-ai-socs-at-near-threshold-why-foundation-ip-can-help-you-break-down-the-power-wall/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Andrew Appleby]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:05:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - LPHP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP And LP In SoCs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[foundation IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[logic libraries]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[low-voltage design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[near-threshold]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[voltage]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[voltage scaling]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284114</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Traditional voltage scaling is no longer sufficient to deliver real-time performance within tight energy and thermal limits.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/designing-edge-ai-socs-at-near-threshold-why-foundation-ip-can-help-you-break-down-the-power-wall/">Designing Edge AI SoCs At Near-Threshold: Why Foundation IP Can Help You Break Down The Power Wall</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Lowering operating voltage is a key to SoC power optimization. In advanced FinFET processes at ~0.4–0.5V, it can also start to become a design risk. As edge AI pushes inference closer to endpoints, edge-AI SoCs must deliver real-time performance within tight energy and thermal limits. Traditional voltage scaling is no longer sufficient.</p>
<p>SoC designs are shifting towards much more extensive use of near-threshold operation to achieve step-function improvements in performance per watt. But this shift fundamentally changes how chips behave: delays increase non-linearly, variability grows significantly, and underpinning predictable operation becomes a critical enabler to minimizing power.</p>
<p>Design success in this operating regime depends heavily on the underlying quality, modeling accuracy, and integration of Foundation IP with EDA flows and silicon validation. Embedded memories, logic libraries, and I/Os must be selected carefully and any targeted optimization carried out and fully validated at an early stage—not treated as a possible downstream option.</p>
<h2>What changes at near-threshold</h2>
<p>Every SoC designer understands that lowering supply voltage reduces energy—but at near threshold voltages, it changes transistor behavior and challenges traditional design assumptions.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284115" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?resize=1419%2C680&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1419" height="680" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?w=1419&amp;ssl=1 1419w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?resize=300%2C144&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?resize=1024%2C491&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?resize=768%2C368&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig1.jpg?resize=600%2C288&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Energy efficiency improves at lower voltage, but delay and variability increase sharply near threshold.</strong></p>
<p>As shown in Figure 1, reducing voltage lowers energy per operation, but comes with significant trade-offs: delay grows rapidly, variability increases, and noise margins shrink.</p>
<p>In this regime:</p>
<ul>
<li>Timing paths become highly sensitive to small voltage variations</li>
<li>IR drop and local voltage droop become first-order effects</li>
<li>Guard-banding becomes less effective</li>
<li>Nominal-voltage models no longer predict behavior accurately</li>
</ul>
<p>Voltage scaling is no longer an isolated optimization—it introduces tightly coupled challenges across the entire design stack.</p>
<h2>Foundation IP: The differentiating layer</h2>
<p>Meeting power targets at ultra-low voltage is not sufficient; designs must achieve this predictably and without introducing additional silicon risk. This can be where Foundation IP moves from an incidental enabler to a significant part of the design success. The quality of logic libraries, memory compilers, and I/Os can directly determine the robustness of design closure and silicon outcomes.</p>
<p>A unified, silicon-proven Foundation IP platform—tightly coupled with EDA flows, built with the most accurate modelling and characterization methodologies and meticulously validated on silicon —provides the accuracy and consistency needed to enable PPA winning designs at ultra-low voltages, with reliable behavior from early design through signoff.</p>
<h3>Embedded memories: Where the voltage floor lives</h3>
<p>Memory is a dominant contributor to area and power—and often determines the system&#8217;s minimum operating voltage (Vmin) if not architecturally decoupled. This is addressed through flexible architectures:</p>
<ul>
<li>Single-rail memories with silicon-validated assist features support operation down to ~600 mV Vmin, enabling direct V² energy reduction without architectural disruption</li>
<li>Dual-rail memories decouple logic and memory voltages, allowing deeper scaling of compute blocks. As shown in Figure 2, this approach has been validated at ISSCC 2025 with a 38 Mb/mm² dual-rail SRAM at 380/540 mV in 3nm FinFET</li>
<li>Ultra-low-power register files support localized data storage in compute-intensive regions</li>
</ul>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284116" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig2.jpg?resize=772%2C679&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="772" height="679" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig2.jpg?w=772&amp;ssl=1 772w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig2.jpg?resize=300%2C264&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig2.jpg?resize=768%2C675&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig2.jpg?resize=600%2C528&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 772px) 100vw, 772px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Dual-rail memory architecture decouples logic and memory VDD, validated at ISSCC 2025 with a 38 Mb/mm² SRAM at 380/540 mV in 3nm FinFET.</strong></p>
<p>Different AI subsystems require different memory strategies: MAC tiles benefit from small, distributed SRAM blocks; activation units from ULP RFs with wide data-paths; and shared system buffers from high-bandwidth AXI interfaces.</p>
<h3>Logic libraries: Accuracy where it matters most</h3>
<p>Logic libraries must be engineered specifically with low-voltage operation in mind:</p>
<ul>
<li>Advanced characterization, such as moment-based LVF timing and noise models across wide bands of PVT coverage</li>
<li>Delay and slew models that capture near-threshold non-linear sensitivities</li>
<li>Enhanced verification checks—including MIS effects, pulse-width integrity, skew interactions, and mixed-device behavior—to address reduced noise margins</li>
<li>Targeted silicon validation, including extended timing and functional sweeps, to ensure model accuracy</li>
</ul>
<p>This modeling accuracy is essential to avoid unexpected surprises during low voltage signoff or timing failures on silicon.</p>
<h3>I/O: Don&#8217;t let the periphery block the core</h3>
<p>In many designs, I/O domains define system-level Vmin. If I/O cannot operate reliably at reduced voltages, it blocks near-threshold logic benefits regardless of how well the core is optimized.</p>
<p>The portfolio spans control interfaces (GPIO, I3C/I2C, SWI3S), high-speed data links (LVDS, RGMII), clock and reference inputs, storage interfaces (SD/eMMC), test I/O, and 2.5D/3D integration I/O for chiplet-based systems—each validated for consistent operation across varying supply levels.</p>
<h2>Co-optimization: Standard IP alone sometimes isn&#8217;t quite enough</h2>
<p>Achieving predictable, optimized operation at ~0.4–0.5V requires tight alignment across Foundation IP, EDA tools, modeling, and silicon validation.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284117" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?resize=1049%2C680&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1049" height="680" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?w=1049&amp;ssl=1 1049w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?resize=300%2C194&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?resize=1024%2C664&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?resize=768%2C498&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys_Edge-AI-SoCs-at-Near-Threshold-fig3.jpg?resize=600%2C389&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: A closed-loop methodology aligns Foundation IP, tools, and silicon feedback to ensure optimized low-voltage design.</strong></p>
<p>As shown in Figure 3, optimal low-voltage design depends on an iterative, closed-loop enablement process:</p>
<ul>
<li>Early exploration of PPA trade-offs, along with specific voltage targeting</li>
<li>Selection of right IP architectures, technology options and configurations, maybe with specific cell tuning</li>
<li>Flow and signoff refinement targeted for low-voltage conditions</li>
<li>Feedback from silicon validation to continually improve accuracy and raise confidence</li>
</ul>
<p>Synopsys Application Engineers bridge the gap between IP/EDA R&amp;D experts and SoC designers during the customer design engagement, ensuring that the features are fully exploited and technologies are optimally deployed. The co-optimization of tools and IP keeps device physics, modeling assumptions, and silicon outcomes tightly coupled—transforming near-threshold design into a low risk, production-ready solution.</p>
<h2>Proof point: ~20% power reduction, zero schedule impact</h2>
<p>A power-sensitive AI networking SoC targeting high volumes in an advanced technology node, using near-threshold design, needed expert enablement. Supporting extremely low operating voltages for a design executed under a very compressed schedule. The Foundation IP solution included specially tuned low-voltage logic cells, custom level shifters and a custom dual-rail memory compiler, with specifically targeted PVT corner set all specifically selected to achieve the optimum design trade-offs for the best power efficiency through near-threshold signoff.</p>
<p>The results:</p>
<ul>
<li>~20% reduction in power consumption</li>
<li>Full achievement of performance and area targets</li>
<li>No schedule impact</li>
<li>Reduced validation complexity while avoiding re-spins</li>
</ul>
<p>The key outcome: predictable execution without schedule or silicon risk.</p>
<h2>End-to-end support for low-voltage adoption</h2>
<p>Synopsys provides comprehensive documentation, design reviews, PPA tuning sessions, and direct application engineering engagement. DTCO and PPA exploration help customers select optimal IP configurations, while custom characterization services extend the platform for specialized requirements.</p>
<h2>The path forward</h2>
<p>As edge AI workloads proliferate, supporting near-threshold operation is becoming a central requirement to enable power-optimized SoC design. Synopsys Foundation IP solutions—logic, memory, and I/O—deliver the architectural rigor, modeling accuracy, and rigorous silicon validation this regime demands. A broad portfolio, with extensive IP sets implemented with design consistency and supporting wide voltage ranges, simplifies IP reuse, enabling easier product migration across process generations and foundries.</p>
<p>Engage Synopsys early to define optimal voltage targets, select the right Foundation IP configurations, and establish a low-voltage-ready methodology tailored to your product goals. The power wall is real—but it&#8217;s not insurmountable.</p>
<p>To learn more, read the white paper “<a href="https://www.synopsys.com/resources/edge-ai-low-voltage-ip.html">Beating the Edge AI Power Wall with Low Voltage Foundation IP</a>.” To explore the full Foundation IP offering, visit our <a href="https://www.synopsys.com/designware-ip/memories-logic-libraries.html">web page.</a></p>
