		<title>IP Integration Challenges Evolve</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/ip-integration-challenges-evolve/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/ip-integration-challenges-evolve/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brian Bailey]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:05:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Agile Analog]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Analog content]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Axiomise]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[chiplet]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[chiplet integration]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[chiplets]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IC Manage]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP integration]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Keysight]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Power and Thermal Control]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[version control]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[VSIA]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283998</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Integration challenges are growing more complex, but the problem is well understood and solutions are emerging.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ip-integration-challenges-evolve/">IP Integration Challenges Evolve</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Integration challenges go beyond making sure a block is properly connected. Semantic issues cause the most problems, and many are due to incomplete or bad specifications.</li>
<li>Complexity, including increasing software content, makes keeping track of everything included in a design more challenging.</li>
<li>Many designs are pushing power and thermal boundaries, and this requires a new layer of information that must be specified and tracked.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>IP integration is not the most glamorous task in semiconductor design, but it often is one of the most troublesome. At the intersection of block-level verification and system-level interactions, bugs are often very difficult to find.</p>
<p>The creation of the Virtual Socket Interface Alliance (VSIA) in 1996 was, for many, the first time the notion of designing a system by integrating third-party IP blocks became a reality. Its charter was to unify and standardize the way IP was constructed, verified, delivered, and supported. The alliance dissolved in 2008, considering its work to either be complete, or transferring parts of it to other organizations that were more focused on specific pieces of the problem. Eighteen years later, the problem continues to evolve.</p>
<p>The idea was that each company should focus on developing the part of the design that was novel and unique, adding their expertise, and allowing the rest to be developed by domain experts.</p>
<p>&#8220;Ten years ago, a design may have had tens of IP blocks,&#8221; says Pedro Pires, SOS product manager at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/keysight-technologies/">Keysight EDA</a>. &#8220;Today, a company may have thousands of IPs, dozens of different versions of the same IP, and you want to make sense of all of this. In addition, they are much more configurable than they were in the past. With the increasing quantity and different types of data now required for each block, it is becoming very difficult to manage in an ad hoc way. &#8220;</p>
<p>This puts a lot of stress on verification. &#8220;The nastiest failures are almost never inside a single block,&#8221; says Ashish Darbari, founder and CEO of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/axiomise/">Axiomise</a>. &#8220;They show up between blocks — message-dependent deadlock, credits that slowly leak away, a reorder buffer that gets corrupted, an ordering rule broken across a bridge. Each block passes its own verification, yet the system hangs because two teams read the same protocol rule slightly differently.&#8221;</p>
<p>Over time, the number of cross-IP issues has increased. &#8220;From an SoC point of view, it could be a lot of different issues, including address mapping, interrupts, coherency, QoS, security,&#8221; says Madhumita Sanyal, senior director for technical product management for HPC IP portfolio at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;Then there are physical implementation scenarios like DVFS, dynamic voltage scaling for lower power. Plus, there is software and operating system interaction. If you consider a die-to-die IP, there&#8217;s interoperability. There is known-good-die package, signal integrity, power integrity, power delivery network, thermal coupling, telemetry, and so forth.&#8221;</p>
<p>While design size has steadily increased, that alone is not an indicator of complexity. &#8220;I may be designing a giant CPU with 32 cores, but that is just 32 copies of the same thing,&#8221; says Dean Drako, CEO of IC Manage. &#8220;It may have a giant cache on it. That is just one cell repeated 100 million times. The complexity is not always proportional to the size of the chip. They are complicated, but the complexity has not linearly grown with the size of the chips. The human brain can&#8217;t deal with it.&#8221;</p>
<p>What matters is how many assumptions a block makes about the world around it, and that number climbs much faster than size. &#8220;A UART needs a clock, a reset, and a register interface, and that&#8217;s about it,&#8221; says Axiomise&#8217;s Darbari. &#8220;A coherent cache, a CHI home node or a NoC carries hundreds of assumptions. There are ordering rules, credit contracts, limits on outstanding transactions, reset and power sequencing, address-map expectations and security attributes. On top of that come configuration parameters whose effects the datasheet often doesn&#8217;t fully explain.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Increasing pressures<br />
</strong>Many areas add to the integration pressures, such as badly written or incomplete specifications for the IP blocks. At the system level, there is increasing software complexity, power and thermal issues – especially when associated with chiplets, increasing analog content, and added complexity with licensing, geopolitical restrictions, and export controls. Each of these will be touched on briefly.</p>
<p><strong>Bad or incomplete specifications: </strong>Specifications are notoriously difficult to make unambiguous or complete. &#8220;The information is oftentimes not complete enough to give you an accurate picture of how it&#8217;s going to behave under all circumstances,&#8221; says Keysight&#8217;s Pires. &#8220;Knowing the conditions in which the system is going to operate will probably help you, depending on the quality of the documentation of your IPs. It might be that the IPs have information that is relevant for that particular set of circumstances, and in that sense, knowing the circumstances where the system is going to operate will help you, but it might not.&#8221;</p>
<p>The problem is often discovered when a bug escapes. &#8220;The architect, the designer, the software team, the DV lead and the formal team all look at the same failure and see something different,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;Nobody can agree on what the system was supposed to do, because nobody ever wrote that down in a form everyone shared. Those differences of opinion were there from day one. They just surface two years later, often in post-silicon debug, when they are most expensive to fix.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Software complexity: </strong>While some software is fully encapsulated and hidden from any external interfaces, much is not. &#8220;Even something as simple as a PHY has a significant software content,&#8221; says Synopsys&#8217; Sanyal. &#8220;There is the boot firmware that will establish all the clocks, resets, and memory. Then the PHY has calibration firmware, which will do the initial calibration and adaptation, and it&#8217;s updated continuously. That driver needs to tie in to the operating system interrupt and the DMA framework, and the BIOS and hypervisor or coherency or IOMMU dependencies for PCI controller and stuff like that. These all extend the dependency to interrupt between topologies and operating system services and power management framework.&#8221;</p>
<p>These issues are problematic enough that the UCIe standard attempted to address it. &#8220;UCIe 3.0 standardizes how firmware gets downloaded early in initialization,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;The trouble is that the hardware is often only correct under some software assumption. For example, registers might have to be programmed in a particular order, or a field must never be written while a transaction is in flight. Those rules are rarely written down, and RTL verification on its own never checks them.&#8221;</p>
<p>The divide between hardware and software is wide. &#8220;The world of software and the world of hardware are very different, meaning different people operate on the hardware side from those that operate on the software side,&#8221; says Pires. &#8220;This includes firmware, software that ties deeply with the IP. The workflows and the cadence in which these two teams work are also different.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Power and thermal: </strong>Controlling power is becoming a larger problem. &#8220;Power management has become a functional feature, and a major source of functional bugs,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;Every power domain adds states, transitions, isolation, and wake-up sequences. A block can be perfectly correct while powered on and still drop a transaction or hang a handshake when its neighbor powers down mid-transfer. Questions like, &#8216;Can this request be lost if that domain switches off while a response is outstanding,&#8217; are classic formal problems. Simulation rarely lands on the exact cycle where it goes wrong.&#8221;</p>
<p>When power is consumed it generates heat, which in turn can alter the behavior of sensitive circuitry. &#8220;With power and thermal now driving SoC limits, analog IP can’t just sit there passively anymore,&#8221; says Chris Morrison, vice president of product marketing at Agile Analog. &#8220;We have to design it to actively manage the chip using embedded PVT sensors, timing monitors, droop detectors, and glitch detectors that feed real-time telemetry back to power management units to catch issues before they affect operation.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Analog content: </strong>Analog content has always had unique challenges. &#8220;These IPs have a limited range of operation, and they&#8217;re very tailored to specific use cases,&#8221; says Pires. &#8220;While AI is helping in many areas, we are seeing a big challenge in creating AI automation to accelerate RF and analog design, because of scarcity of data. It&#8217;s not so easy to come by a good quality data set with enough volume that you can use to train an agent to do a specific part of your flow. It&#8217;s much more tailored, focused, narrow information, and oftentimes it is proprietary information not available for us to consume.&#8221;</p>
<p>Analog block creators are looking for ways to abstract their designs into a digital world. &#8220;Customers are increasingly asking for complete analog subsystems rather than wrestling with a dozen individual blocks,&#8221; says Agile Analog&#8217;s Morrison. &#8220;Simulating large analog subsystems in SPICE takes far too long at the SoC level, so verification can be simplified by insulating the IP with a digital wrapper. Doing the heavy mixed-signal lifting internally means digital SoC integration teams can take SystemVerilog real-number models (RNMs) early on and run full-chip verification within their digital environment.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Chiplets: </strong>Chiplets can be viewed as a new physical delivery mechanism for IP. &#8220;Chiplets magnify that issue and make it a supply chain integration issue too,&#8221; says Frank Schirrmeister, executive director of strategic programs for system solutions at Synopsys. &#8220;You not only have the IP issues, but you move the design up from the SoC boundary to the packaging boundary. Now it turns IP integration into supply chain integration. You&#8217;re not only integrating the designs, but you&#8217;re also integrating all these bits that may come from different companies, nodes, and foundries, and they all have their own verification methodologies at the IP level.&#8221;</p>
<p>None of the integration issues go away. &#8220;NoCs are now being stretched across die boundaries, so several dies look like one fabric,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;UCIe is handling the physical and link layers well, and cross-vendor interoperability is being demonstrated. But a working link doesn&#8217;t make a working system. You still need agreement on address maps, coherency models and software, and those are exactly the semantic questions standards leave open. The specs now include DFD, DFT, and DFM, and manageability, so a chiplet starts to look less like a passive block and more like a small system with its own management firmware.&#8221;</p>
<p>Chiplets need even more information than an IP block. &#8220;There are now multi-physics coming into play, which means that you need a whole lot more information about the IPs that you&#8217;re managing,&#8221; says Pires. &#8220;It&#8217;s just becoming unwieldy to have all of this data together. It means more complexity that travels with your IP. For example, the power intent needs to travel with your IP as an additional artifact.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Verification: </strong>With IP, and even more so with chiplets, visibility into the block makes verification more difficult. &#8220;Large IP increasingly arrives encrypted, so you can&#8217;t read the RTL to work out what it really expects,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;You&#8217;re left with the documentation, and in my experience that is often where the bug is. These blocks are also shipped as generators rather than fixed designs. The vendor verified some set of configurations, but you are probably taping out a different one. With more than a handful of parameters, it is very easy to land on a combination nobody tested.&#8221;</p>
<p>The good news should be that not every capability of an IP needs to be verified. &#8220;Identifying the legal subsets of functionality is an architecture and verification task,&#8221; says Sanyal. &#8220;It&#8217;s not one-size-fits-all that can be automated. If you look at the verification aspect of it, there are now AI models that we are using, our customers are using, to get into those corner cases faster than before. It&#8217;s getting into the verification part of it.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Version control<br />
</strong>Very few things are perfect on the first attempt. Bugs will be fixed after the initial release. &#8220;The non-AI version is this is about IP lifecycle management,&#8221; says IC Manage&#8217;s Drako. &#8220;With AI, those life cycles may move faster, but you still need to manage those lifecycles. And maybe you can ask AI to do it for you, but you must have the data and the systems in place to keep track of it, know what you taped out, know what you built, know what was used where, which IP was used where.&#8221;</p>
<p>Hardware and software ship and update on different schedules. &#8220;A firmware update in the field can change how silicon behaves after it was signed off,&#8221; says Darbari. &#8220;Interrupts, power states, DMA buffers, and security attributes are shared between hardware and software, and conflicts tend to surface at bring-up when the first real driver runs a sequence nobody specified.&#8221;</p>
<p>Hardware and software generally use different mechanisms for version control. &#8220;The challenge has to do with integrating all of this data within a single system,&#8221; says Pires. &#8220;A specific version of your hardware needs to correspond to a specific version of the software, and this is a link that exists often in Excel spreadsheets, which is messy and error-prone. When you are integrating a system, you need to manage the configuration itself, like the bill of materials for a specific system, the specific combination of components that you have in a system. Every time that you do a change to it, you need it to persist. You need to save it so that you have that traceability aspect.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion<br />
</strong>It might seem as if this is a growing cause for alarm. But like most problems within the EDA space, as a problem grows, so does the attention it receives, and the investment in the problem ultimately leads to better solutions.</p>
<p>&#8220;The management problem is growing, but also the mechanisms to address the problem evolve alongside it,&#8221; says Pires. &#8220;In relative terms, the pain remains constant, but the problem underlying it is a bigger one.&#8221;</p>
<p><i>Editor&#8217;s note: A second part of this report will examine recent improvements and how AI is being used to improve the selection, configuration, and integration of IP.</i></p>
<hr />

		<title>The Memory Challenge Of Physical AI</title>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Right-size the memory hierarchy around the device's data streams, reuse patterns, latency requirements, and operating constraints.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-memory-challenge-of-physical-ai/">The Memory Challenge Of Physical AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Most AI hardware conversations start with compute, which is understandable since accelerators are visible, easy to compare, and often tied to headline performance metrics. Physical AI broadens that conversation by placing greater emphasis on how compute, memory, and data movement work together under real-world operating constraints. For intelligent systems that must see, interpret, decide, and act in the physical world in real time, it’s critical that the right data reaches the right engine at the right time.</p>
<p>As a result, data movement has become an increasingly important consideration when architecting physical AI systems. A physical AI device may simultaneously handle sensor streams, model weights, activations, temporary buffers, control loops, and safety functions. If memory access becomes unpredictable, compute engines can stall. Power consumption rises when data is moved too often, and poorly planned buffering can cause latency accumulation that is hard to recover from later.</p>
<p>As physical AI workloads become more complex, architects must now consider how predictably the complete system performs when multiple real-world workloads are active simultaneously.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284053" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig1.jpg?resize=845%2C430&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="845" height="430" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig1.jpg?w=845&amp;ssl=1 845w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig1.jpg?resize=300%2C153&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig1.jpg?resize=768%2C391&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig1.jpg?resize=600%2C305&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 845px) 100vw, 845px" /></p>
<h2>Avoid unnecessary data movement</h2>
<p>Physical AI systems rely on a memory hierarchy that coordinates on-chip memory with larger-capacity external memory. LPDDR or GDDR may hold model weights, sensor data, activations, and other working data, while on-chip SRAM and cache keep the portions currently needed by the NPU, CPU, DSP, ISP, or other processing engines close to compute.</p>
<p>As workloads move through the processing pipeline, required data is transferred from external memory into on-chip memory, where it can be accessed and reused with lower latency and energy. The more effectively the architecture reuses that local data, the fewer times it must retrieve the same information from external memory. However, because SRAM and cache consume silicon area, their capacity is limited. Data that does not fit on-chip, or is used less frequently, remains in LPDDR or GDDR until it is needed.</p>
<p>This coordination is important because every unnecessary transfer across the memory hierarchy consumes energy, adds latency, and uses external-memory bandwidth. Physical AI systems are also constrained by device size, package complexity, power delivery, and thermal headroom. The goal is therefore not to eliminate external memory access, but to keep the right data close to compute for as long as it is useful and avoid moving it more often than necessary.</p>
<p>A well-planned dataflow coordinates compute engines, on-chip SRAM and cache, external LPDDR or GDDR, buffering, and interconnect around the workload. This helps keep compute resources productive while using external-memory capacity and bandwidth efficiently.</p>
<h2>Real-world applications clarify the tradeoffs</h2>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284054" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=1101%2C517&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1101" height="517" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?w=1101&amp;ssl=1 1101w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=300%2C141&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=1024%2C481&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=768%2C361&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=600%2C282&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p>Humanoid robots, drones, autonomous vehicles, smart cameras, inspection robots, industrial robots, and autonomous mobile robots may all run local intelligence, yet each stresses memory and interfaces differently. Their architectures must determine which data should remain close to compute for immediate access and which should reside in higher-capacity external memory.</p>
<p>Humanoids may combine cameras, depth sensing, audio, inertial data, balance control, object recognition, path planning, and actuator feedback within a limited power and thermal envelope. If perception data arrives late, control decisions may be delayed. In addition, sustained performance drops and power rises if model weights and intermediate data are reloaded too often. In this kind of system, memory efficiency affects how smoothly the robot can perceive, decide, and move, all of which are key to successful real-time operation.</p>
<p>A drone or autonomous mobile robot has a different set of constraints. It may need visual navigation, obstacle avoidance, stabilization, object tracking, wireless communication, and video processing, all while staying within a limited power and thermal budget. Unnecessary external memory accesses reduce operating time, add heat, or introduce latency into the control loop. For these physical AI systems, bandwidth per watt is as important as raw bandwidth.</p>
<p>Autonomous vehicles and advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS) present an even greater challenge. With cameras, radar, lidar, ultrasonic sensors, vehicle telemetry, map data, and control functions, competition for memory access is fierce. Some data streams need continuous throughput, while others need fusion across sensor domains. Retaining relevant data long enough to support planning and decision-making is also an important consideration. In this environment, memory behavior affects both throughput and response predictability.</p>
<h2>Memory choices must follow the workload</h2>
<p>When building a memory architecture, architects should begin with the behavior of the physical AI workload itself. The objective is not simply to select the fastest external memory technology or provision the greatest possible bandwidth. It is to right-size the complete memory hierarchy, including local storage, external memory capacity, bandwidth, power, buffering, and integration, around the device’s data streams, reuse patterns, latency requirements, and operating constraints.</p>
<p>LPDDR and GDDR address different architectural priorities. LPDDR is often a strong fit when bandwidth per watt and memory capacity are needed to sustain local inference performance. When continuous image or sensor-stream bandwidth is the primary requirement, GDDR may be a viable option because its high bandwidth can meet those performance needs with fewer DRAM devices.</p>
<p>Available solutions give architects considerable flexibility in making this tradeoff. For example, Cadence offers LPDDR6/LPDDR5X PHY and Controller IP supporting data rates up to 14.4Gbps, as well as GDDR7 PHY IP supporting data rates up to 36Gbps for high-bandwidth applications. These options allow the external memory interface to be aligned with the workload rather than treated as a one-size-fits-all design choice.</p>
<p>A humanoid robot may prioritize power-efficient access to model weights, perception data, and control information. A smart camera or autonomous-vehicle perception processor may place greater pressure on sustained vision-stream bandwidth. In each case, the architecture should keep frequently reused and latency-sensitive data close to compute while selecting an external memory interface sized for the remaining capacity and bandwidth requirements. This helps avoid both memory bottlenecks and unnecessary over-provisioning.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284055" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=722%2C406&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="722" height="406" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig3.jpg?w=722&amp;ssl=1 722w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=300%2C169&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=600%2C337&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 722px) 100vw, 722px" /></p>
<table style="border-collapse: collapse;" border="1">
<thead>
<tr style="background-color: #ddd;">
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Device context</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Examples</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Primary pressure</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Likely memory fit</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Mobile physical AI devices</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Humanoids, drones, autonomous mobile robots, robotic modules</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Sustained local inference under size, capacity, bandwidth, and latency limits</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LPDDR for bandwidth-per-watt and high DRAM capacity design; GDDR selectively for heavy vision streams</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Vision-heavy physical AI devices</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Autonomous vehicles, smart cameras, inspection robots, industrial vision systems</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Continuous image or sensor streams with predictable response</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LPDDR where memory capacity matters; GDDR when stream bandwidth dominates</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2>Five memory architecture decisions</h2>
<p>Before selecting an external memory interface, architects should evaluate how the device’s dataflow and operating constraints affect five key areas:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Data locality:</strong> Which data should remain close to compute to meet latency, reuse, and energy-efficiency requirements, and which data should reside in external memory?</li>
<li><strong>Traffic contention:</strong> Where will sensor, inference, software, safety, and control traffic contend for memory access?</li>
<li><strong>External memory fit:</strong> Which external memory option best matches the device’s bandwidth, power, and integration needs?</li>
<li><strong>Buffering strategy:</strong> How much local storage and buffering are needed to reuse data effectively and avoid unnecessary off-chip movement?</li>
<li><strong>Latency predictability:</strong> Can the device keep latency bounded when multiple workloads are active?</li>
</ul>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284056" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=864%2C320&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="864" height="320" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig4.jpg?w=864&amp;ssl=1 864w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=300%2C111&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=768%2C284&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence_Memory-Challenge-Physical-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=600%2C222&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 864px) 100vw, 864px" /></p>
<p>A balanced memory architecture aligns data locality, traffic management, external memory selection, buffering, and latency predictability with the device workload. Together, these decisions help reduce unnecessary data movement, keep compute resources productive, and maintain predictable performance when multiple workloads operate concurrently.</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>Physical AI places intelligence inside devices that operate continuously, interact with real-world signals, and respond under power, thermal, latency, and reliability constraints. Meeting these demands requires compute, memory, and data movement to function as a coordinated system. Predictable data movement is crucial for physical AI devices to operate effectively under real-world conditions, making memory architecture an important part of the overall design. Selecting the right memory requires a careful balance of latency, power efficiency, bandwidth, buffering, and workload requirements. Architects must keep frequently used data close to the engines that need it, use external bandwidth efficiently, keep compute resources productive, and maintain predictable system behavior as workloads operate concurrently.</p>
<p>Learn more about how to architect physical AI systems with design IP in Cadence’s <a href="https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/resources/white-papers/designing-physical-ai-systems-under-real-world-constraints.html">“Designing Physical AI Systems Under Real-World Constraints”</a> white paper.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-memory-challenge-of-physical-ai/">The Memory Challenge Of Physical AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Introduction To FP8 Formats: Part 1</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/introduction-to-fp8-formats-part-1/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/introduction-to-fp8-formats-part-1/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Siyu Mou]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:03:25 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[At The Core]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Blogs - LPHP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[8-bit floating point]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ARM]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[floating point]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[FP8]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[number formats]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284065</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>The rationale behind 8-bit floating-point data formats, their ecosystem momentum, and hardware support.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/introduction-to-fp8-formats-part-1/">Introduction To FP8 Formats: Part 1</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>The industry is moving from high-precision formats (FP32, FP16, BF16) to lower-precision data types for Machine Learning (ML) inference and training. In cloud and data center environments, lower-precision data types deliver higher throughput by packing more compute per cycle from the same platform. On edge and mobile devices, smaller formats reduce model size, memory footprint and bandwidth, which directly affects power consumption and latency.</p>
<p>Arm, Intel, and NVIDIA published an OCP 8-bit Floating Point Specification (OFP8). It is an interchange format that allows software systems to share the models easily. FP8 is not a future concept, it is being deployed today. It is already supported by NVIDIA’s Hopper H100 GPU, with software deployment appearing in major ML frameworks.</p>
<p>Arm added the FP8 support to the Arm v9 ISA and is exploring the best way to integrate the FP8 across all the ML platforms.</p>
<p>This blog post explains the FP8 data formats, the new Floating-point Mode Register (FPMR), and the instruction set that makes FP8 practical on Arm CPUs.</p>
<h2>FP8 data format</h2>
<p>Unlike IEEE FP64, FP32, and FP16, FP8 is not a single format, it is a family. The whitepaper <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.05433">FP8 Formats for Deep Learning</a> proposes an FP8 format consisting of two encodings: E4M3 and E5M2.</p>
<h3>Why a family of formats?</h3>
<p>With only 8 bits available, FP8 makes a trade-off between precision and dynamic range. A single encoding cannot fit all tensors equally well. Activations and weights usually have a smaller and more stable value distribution after training or calibration. They can therefore benefit from more mantissa bits, which help represent nearby values more precisely. Gradients can change much more during forward and backward passes. They can therefore benefit from more exponent bits and a larger dynamic range, which helps avoid overflow or underflow.</p>
<p>The two encodings enable software to choose the right format for each tensor role. Use E4M3 when precision is more important and use E5M2 when dynamic range is more important. This is the key idea from FP8, and Arm reflects the same idea in the FPMR register. With FPMR, each source operand can independently select which FP8 format it uses.</p>
<h3>Supported data types</h3>
<table style="border-collapse: collapse;" border="1">
<tbody>
<tr style="background-color: #ddd;">
<td style="padding: 5px;"></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>E4M3</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>E5M2</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Exponent bits</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">4</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Mantissa bits</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">3</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Bias</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">7</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">15</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Max normal value</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">448</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">57344</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Special values</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">NaN only</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Inf and NaN</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>E4M3 uses 1 sign bit, 4 exponent bits, and 3 mantissa bits, with a bias of 7. E4M3 uses most bit patterns for normal finite numbers instead of saving many of them for special values. This helps E4M3 extend dynamic range, which is more useful for deep learning workloads.</p>
<p>E5M2 uses 1 sign bit, 5 exponent bits, and 2 mantissa bits, with a bias of 15. E5M2 follows IEEE 754 conventions for the exponent field and special values, including infinities and NaNs. Because of this, it can be viewed as IEEE FP16 with fewer mantissa bits, like how bfloat16 and TF32 can be viewed as IEEE FP32 with fewer bits. This makes conversion between E5M2 and IEEE FP16 straightforward, while still providing a wider dynamic range than E4M3</p>
<h2>FPMR: The Floating-Point Mode Register</h2>
<p>When hardware executes an FP8 instruction, it must know the format of each source operand, the format of the destination result, and the scale factors to apply.</p>
<p>Because FP8 modes can change per tensor, software may need to update the format and scaling settings frequently inside performance-critical code. For this reason, Arm introduced a dedicated control register &#8211; FPMR. FPMR is separate from FPCR. Hardware can recognize and rename FPMR writes more efficiently, making per-tensor FP8 mode changes closer to a normal register write rather than a pipeline-serializing control update.</p>
<p>FPMR contains the following fields:</p>
<table style="border-collapse: collapse;" border="1">
<thead>
<tr style="background-color: #ddd;">
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Field</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Bits</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Description</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">F8S1</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[2:0]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Format of 1st FP8 source. 0b000 = E5M2, 0b001 = E4M3.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">F8S2</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[5:3]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Format of 2nd FP8 source. Same encoding as F8S1.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">F8D</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[8:6]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Destination result Format for instructions that convert other floating-point formats to FP8.<br />
Same encoding as F8S1.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">OSM</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[14]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Overflow Saturation for FP8 multiplication instructions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">OSC</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[15]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Overflow Saturation for FP8 convert instructions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LSCALE</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[22:16]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Unsigned Log2 down-scaling factor. Result × 2^(−LSCALE).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">NSCALE</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[31:24]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Unsigned Log2 up-scaling factor for widening FP8 sources.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LSCALE2</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">[37:32]</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Second down-scaling value for dual-source convert instructions.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h2>FP8 instruction set on Arm</h2>
<p>The 2023 and 2024 architecture extensions add FP8 instructions across Advanced SIMD, SVE and SME. The following table summarizes the architectural features and the instructions they provide.</p>
<table style="border-collapse: collapse;" border="1">
<thead>
<tr style="background-color: #ddd;">

<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Supported category</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Instructions</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Description</strong></td>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FEAT_FP8</td>

a floating-point scale instruction.</td>

<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FEAT_SSVE_FP8DOT2</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">SME</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FDOT (.H)</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FP8 → FP16 2-way dot product and accumulate in Streaming SVE mode.</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td style="padding: 5px;">SME</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FMOPA, FDOT, FVDOTB, FVDOTT, FMLALL</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FP8 outer product and accumulate into FP32 ZA tile;<br />
FP8 dot product and accumulate into FP32 ZA array;<br />
FP8 widening multiply-accumulate into FP32 ZA array;</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FEAT_SME_F8F16</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">SME</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FMOPA, FDOT, FVDOT, FMLAL</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FP8 outer product and accumulate into FP16 ZA tile;<br />
FP8 dot product and accumulate into FP16 ZA array;<br />
FP8 widening multiply-accumulate into FP16 ZA array.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FEAT_F8F32MM</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Advanced SIMD/SVE</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FMMLA</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FP8 matrix multiply-accumulate to FP32</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FEAT_F8F16MM</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Advanced SIMD/SVE</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FMMLA</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">FP8 matrix multiply-accumulate to FP16</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>All FP8 compute instructions read the source format from FPMR (F8S1/F8S2) and apply scaling via LSCALE/NSCALE. These widening instructions always use FP8 data as the input format. Accumulation uses a wider type, such as FP16 or FP32. This keeps the bandwidth advantage of FP8 inputs while preserving numerical stability during accumulation.</p>
<h3>Further reading</h3>
<p>For more detailed information about FP8, see the <a href="https://support.arm.com/documentation/ddi0487/md/">Arm Architecture Reference Manual for A-profile architecture</a> and the FP8 Formats for Deep Learning <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.05433">paper</a>.</p>
<p>Part 2 will explore FP8 instructions, from conversion and scaling to dot products, matrix multiplication, and outer products in Advanced SIMD, SVE, and SME.</p>

		<item>
		<title>RISC-V For AI: Breakthrough Or Bad Idea?</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/risc-v-for-ai-breakthrough-or-bad-idea/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/risc-v-for-ai-breakthrough-or-bad-idea/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ann Mutschler]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:01:26 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Round Tables]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Breker]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Quadric]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[RISC-V]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Signature IP]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284039</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Custom instructions promise flexibility, but experts question whether RISC-V belongs at the heart of AI acceleration. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/risc-v-for-ai-breakthrough-or-bad-idea/">RISC-V For AI: Breakthrough Or Bad Idea?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>RISC-V’s role in AI is contested. Its customization offers flexibility, but splitting inference workloads between CPUs and accelerators can introduce costly data movement and compiler complexity.</li>
<li>Control may be RISC-V’s best option. Custom instructions can help orchestrate accelerators, reduce load/store latency, and handle limited mathematical tasks without taking on heavyweight inference.</li>
<li>Correct instructions don’t guarantee a working chip. Custom AI systems also need observability and system-level verification to expose performance bottlenecks, cache-coherency problems, and interconnect deadlocks.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p><strong>Experts At The Table:</strong> Demand for parallelism and power efficiency is shifting chip design from general computing to AI, increasing the need for flexible, modular, and open hardware. This trend is bringing RISC-V into AI acceleration. Customizable hardware can implement AI computations directly instead of relying on fixed CPU designs. Semiconductor Engineering sat down to discuss these issues with Steve Roddy, chief marketing officer at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/quadric/">Quadric</a>; Francisca Tan, director of product management and product engineering for Tessent at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mentor-a-siemens-business/">Siemens EDA</a>; Ewald Liess, senior vice president of sales and business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/signatureip/">SignatureIP</a>; and Dave Kelf, CEO of Breker. What follows are excerpts of that conversation.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284041" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=1714%2C472&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1714" height="472" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?w=1714&amp;ssl=1 1714w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=300%2C83&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=1024%2C282&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=768%2C211&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=1536%2C423&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-11-at-11.04.52-AM.png?resize=600%2C165&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>L-R: Quadric’s Roddy; Siemens EDA’s Tan; SignatureIP’s Liess; Breker&#8217;s Kelf.</strong></p>
<p><strong>SE: With AI models evolving rapidly, architects use RISC-V to design highly specialized extensions. From an IP provider standpoint, how would you balance delivering a standardized, reusable IP core with the extreme customization that customers are demanding for AI workloads? </strong></p>
<p><strong>Leiss</strong>: We don&#8217;t think that we need to configure because we provide NoC and PCI Express and CXL CHI AMBA interfaces. Based on this, we don&#8217;t need to customize. Instead, it&#8217;s the NoC that will be configured. Because we have a pre-verified NoC, we can do that with our tools. We can configure customized RISC-Vs. Of course, the customers may modify the RISC-V core with new instructions, but they will stick to the interfaces like the AMBA bus, or CXL, PCI Express interfaces, because this enables them to access the external components that they need, like memory or going off-chip. And this is where we come in. So the RISC-V guys should feel free to do whatever they want.</p>
<p><strong>Tan</strong>: Custom verification and validation also become important, as does observability. For test and embedded analytics, engineers doing debug or silicon bring-up can monitor, introspect, and inspect internal signals, or calculate whether performance is being met. In my view, it&#8217;s also about hardware observability, as the industry moves into various challenges and complexity in the chip.</p>
<p><strong>Roddy</strong>: We&#8217;ll take the contrarian view in this particular area. We think that starting with RISC-V to do an AI accelerator is a flat-out bad idea, because you instantly create a problem. You now have a partition between some things running on a programmable processor with good ALU capacity, and some external accelerator where you&#8217;ve now had to partition the system. The minute you partition, the minute you create a problem, you need fancy compilers to go fix it and figure out how to move data from the accelerator or accelerators across a nice NoC over to a programmable core, and back and forth. That&#8217;s a problem that scales badly. The bigger the accelerator, the worse it gets. And so now you&#8217;re compounding the problem. If you have something that&#8217;s, say, 100 TOPS and you have to have, say, 16 RISC-V processors slave to a whole bunch of accelerator blocks, sure, you need great interconnect, and you need great verification, and all those things that go with it because you&#8217;re trying to solve the underlying fundamental problem, which is starting with a CPU. And CPUs are great for chasing pointers. That&#8217;s what CPUs do. CPUs have wonderful logic to handle the fact that you&#8217;ve got 40 threads running, and on the very next clock tick, you have no idea what you&#8217;ll be running. That&#8217;s not what you&#8217;re doing when you&#8217;re doing AI inference. You know for millions of cycles in advance what&#8217;s happening. You don&#8217;t need most of the CPU hardware. DSPs are a little closer to what you need, but DSPs are vector machines — vector-by-scalar or vector-by-vector. And most of what is challenging in AI is matrix-by-matrix. So we built a matrix-native machine. The only other one I know of that&#8217;s close to that is in the data center, and that&#8217;s the big giant TPUs from Google. Literally, everybody else has a partitioned problem, and then they struggle and spend billions of dollars trying to handle that partitioning problem and figure out how to make up for the fact that they started from, we think, a bad place.</p>
<p><strong>Kelf</strong>: I agree with Steve&#8217;s point, because what we&#8217;re seeing is large AI accelerators — which might be different architectures, SIMD, SIMT — are pretty common. These kinds of things basically are controlled, or have been controlled, by a complex state machine of some sort, and then there&#8217;ll be some kind of host processor driving several of these different things and telling the state machine what to do, and then driving the accelerator. What we&#8217;ve seen in four instances now, both big and small companies, is that the state machines are hopeless. So they&#8217;re taking a RISC-V processor, and they&#8217;re using that as the control function on top of these accelerators for a number of reasons. One is that they can, in fact, build in a bunch of mathematical engines — exactly what Steve was mentioning — vector type, using either the RISC-V vector instructions or those. And then they add in their own, as well, or some of the new matrix multiplication instructions. They&#8217;re also putting in their own instructions for very efficient load/store to reduce the latency that you see between partitions. So they&#8217;re trying to figure out how they can adjust these RISC-V processes to reduce that latency and to reduce that partitioning problem. We have a number of examples where some are successful, some are less so. They&#8217;re still working on this problem, but RISC-V is nice because they can add whatever custom instructions they want, still use a regular compiler, and create a mechanism to drive a very efficient load/store that they can&#8217;t with other processors. Then, of course, there are people like Arm and others who have built instructions into some of their processors, but they&#8217;re not variable. RISC-V is ideal for this, and pretty unique in its ability to create these powerful custom instructions.</p>
<p><strong>SE: Regarding those custom vector instructions, are EDA tools accounting for this and automating the verification?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Roddy</strong>: I spent 17 years at Tensilica. We automated all that. The downside, of course, is that you pay a royalty. RISC-V is an opportunity to not pay a royalty, a tribute, a tax to the big guy. If you want customization, the folks at Tensilica automated that many years ago. The idea is to change instructions and instantly get compilers and simulators that match, with verification handled because it&#8217;s in a structured environment. RISC-V gives a bit more flexibility to go outside those bounds. My take on that is if you really want to use RISC-V, you better be going all out and customizing the hell out of it to go through the effort to manually create all that stuff, because if you want just a little bit of customization, my old friends at Tensilica have an automated system that makes that happen. Yes, it comes with a commercial cost. RISC-V is a very interesting technology, and it absolutely serves a purpose. The market leader doesn&#8217;t allow that level of flexibility for control and CPU functions. You can get a lot of value out of tweaking and tuning a processor for the control function at hand. We just don&#8217;t think it makes sense to do inference on it. But there are thousands of things that CPUs are used for, and RISC-V CPUs can do those things perhaps more flexibly and more efficiently, and certainly at a lower price point than fixed-function CPUs from other vendors. We just don&#8217;t think doing heavy lifting and inferencing is the right way to deploy that kind of technology.</p>
<p><strong>Kelf</strong>: I tend to agree. Some companies are trying to build in the vectors and build their own mathematical sort of DSP-type instructions. I was talking with some customers who were having some issues. They had a SIMT array — single-instruction, multiple threads — and they were doing a ton of parallel mathematics and hoping one of the parallel lanes wasn&#8217;t going to diverge from the others. And then one does. The nice thing about SIMT is you can make it diverge. They were using the RISC-V control processor to do some of the mathematical processing from the divergence, leaving the rest of the accelerator to do the rest of the mathematical processing that&#8217;s going on. They&#8217;re not doing heavyweight processing inside the RISC-V. They&#8217;re just doing enough to keep the whole machine working and reduce that latency, but doing this odd thing inside the RISC-V. It took them a lot of work to get this working properly, but now they are seeing this as quite successful. I agree that there&#8217;s the business side of RISC-V, which is you don&#8217;t have to pay a royalty, and I agree on the Tensilica technology as well, which was fantastic for this kind of stuff, especially DSP communications. But they can do similar things inside RISC-V. They have to build it themselves. They have to know how to do that. But once they do, there&#8217;s a lot of flexibility, a lot of standardization around RISC-V. They can get a compiler. They can potentially shift to a different company and build some of their proprietary stuff into that RISC-V. That does allow for more flexibility, but more standardization, as well, which you may not get out of a fixed processor and a proprietary processor.</p>
<p><strong>Tan</strong>: In terms of what testing and better analytics provide for custom instructions, especially from the RISC-V arena, we support debuggability for custom-instruction tracing. We support RISC-V users specifying which custom instructions they want to trace while the processor is running, to support that debuggability environment. We are also one of the members of the RISC-V organization, whereby we donated a few years back the Efficient Trace (E-Trace) specification. That is a highly compressed instruction trace catering to complex systems where there&#8217;s going to be a lot of data, and therefore we need to think about compressing all this data as well. Within our infrastructure, it&#8217;s not just about the processor. It&#8217;s about the entire system. So our infrastructure can capture instruction traces from the RISC-V core, as well as from various parts of the RISC-V SoC, such as the interconnect, bus monitoring, or specific signals we want to monitor.</p>
<p><strong>Leiss</strong>: We’ve talked a lot about verifying the custom instruction set, passing the ISA-level verification, and most of the time, maybe generated IP comes with verification collateral like test benches, traffic generators, and performance models. But I look at it from a system point of view, and it means that there are typically multiple cores in one high-performance system, and when you have an accelerator in RISC-V five, or you have several RISC-V in one, then you immediately end up with a coherency problem because you use caches and so on. The coherency corner cases and traffic bursts can easily cause deadlocks in the fabric. It is therefore important that, from the NoC side, we do all this verification at the system level to check how the traffic can flow through and make sure that you don&#8217;t end up in a deadlock situation. In simple terms, an instruction can execute correctly, but the chip can still fail if the data burst needed for the instruction or the calculation isn&#8217;t available. That&#8217;s an area we&#8217;re addressing.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/risc-v-for-ai-breakthrough-or-bad-idea/">RISC-V For AI: Breakthrough Or Bad Idea?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Foundation IP For The AI Era: Why Execution Shapes SoC Cost ─ eBook</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/foundation-ip-for-the-ai-era-why-execution-shapes-soc-cost/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/foundation-ip-for-the-ai-era-why-execution-shapes-soc-cost/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 08:01:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Low Power-High Performance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Whitepapers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[foundation IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284049</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How an integrated foundation IP strategy can reduce execution risk, enable faster tape-out, and lower TCO. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/foundation-ip-for-the-ai-era-why-execution-shapes-soc-cost/">Foundation IP For The AI Era: Why Execution Shapes SoC Cost ─ eBook</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>As AI workloads push power, performance, voltage, and process variation to new limits, the economics of advanced-node SoCs are changing. In addition to the cost of the IP itself, hidden costs often arrive later in the design cycle: integration delays, schedule slips, re-spins, and missed market windows. As design complexity rises, predictable execution is becoming a critical factor in total cost of ownership (TCO) alongside power, performance, and area (PPA).</p>
<p>Sourcing Foundation IP piece by piece can add another layer of complexity. Logic libraries, embedded memories, I/Os, and OTP NVM IP from multiple suppliers bring different deliverables, roadmaps, support models, and integration requirements. An integrated Foundation IP platform can reduce these seams by bringing the core building blocks together as one silicon-proven, verified stack, with sign-off-ready characterization and IP + EDA co-optimization to improve PPA and accelerate implementation. This tighter alignment helps design teams move toward tape-out with greater predictability.</p>
<p>The impact extends beyond integration. Across HPC and Edge AI designs, improvements in PPA can translate into meaningful economic value through smaller die area, lower operating power, and increased performance, while avoiding a re-spin or major tape-out delay can protect even more value. The Synopsys eBook <strong>“</strong><a href="https://www.synopsys.com/resources/foundation-ip-ebook.html">Foundation IP for the AI Era</a><strong>”</strong> explores these tradeoffs through 3nm data center and Edge AI SoC examples, combining silicon-proven Synopsys Foundation IP PPA benchmarks with modeled cost estimates based on public market data to illustrate how execution decisions can affect TCO.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.synopsys.com/resources/foundation-ip-ebook.html">Download the eBook</a> to see how an integrated Foundation IP strategy can help reduce execution risk, enable faster tape-out, and lower TCO for HPC and Edge AI SoCs.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/foundation-ip-for-the-ai-era-why-execution-shapes-soc-cost/">Foundation IP For The AI Era: Why Execution Shapes SoC Cost ─ eBook</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
